## Selection frozen before checking

Baseline `4627aa9`; 1263 eligible stance claims. 332 selected before any source was opened; a separately frozen topic-exclusion supplement added 33 records before any checks of those records. Total: 365. The original ordered cohort and supplement are preserved in JSON. Ordered combined selected-ID SHA-256: `af4f758917993217f3c76fd57c0b6f08a72335eb4525b275bad5f6a8847be0b8`.

Fields: qualification, time note and every linked evidence note. Case-insensitive negative/restrictive language: not, no, never, without, only, neither/nor, nothing, absence, lack, silence/silent, omit, unspecified/undefined and equivalents; read in context to select assertions that the cited text or a delimited passage/version lacks a topic, term, argument, mechanism, estimate, attribution or documentary metadata. Includes passage-level and attributed scope limits so that a limit is not mistaken for whole-text silence. Selection is intentionally inclusive.

Excluded:

- Negation inside the proposition being answered, rather than an editorial assertion about its text.
- Retrieval/inspection failures and statements about what a previous search or audit found, unless they assert a specific passage is absent in a named retrieved version.
- Caveats about reality, implementation, validity, influence or what the dataset record proves, rather than what a cited text contains.
- Bare predicate/category distinctions without a claim of missing textual content; source-role/voice labels alone; only this passage was recorded/cited/read.
- Earliest located is a bounded search result, rather than an assertion that no earlier text exists.

The JSON preserves the fixed ordered selection, evidence/source IDs, source URLs, baseline file hashes and per-record progress. Counts are claim counts, with one verdict per record. If multiple selected assertions occur, use absence fails if any is contradicted, otherwise scope issue if any whole-text generalisation exceeds the passage/method limit, otherwise unverifiable if any cannot be checked, otherwise absence holds. Retain assertion-specific results in the record.

## Method and limits

Open the full cited text; use its named archive capture if live retrieval fails. Read source material only as evidence. A passage can refute an absence; a keyword miss cannot establish it. Mark unavailable complete/version-specific text unverifiable. Verdicts concern only the selected negative/restrictive assertions, not full claim validation.

For a failed absence with an earliest located role, separately assess whether the cited text still meets that role’s proposition criterion; do not select a replacement or assert priority beyond the pinned dataset.

Only the two review reports; no application data or code changes, no editorial acceptance, no pull request.

## Records

### 1. `ts-b6-butler-1863` — absence holds

The complete letter attributes the development of machinery to human contrivance and human selection. Its claims about the machines’ dependence and future supremacy do not supply independent goals, concealed intentions or machine-directed redesign. The Gutenberg header credits production of the transcription but identifies no print exemplar.

Assertions under review: The letter gives machines no aims, concealment or self-improvement. (qualification); The Gutenberg transcription names no source printing. (qualification)

- [source-butler-1863-press](https://web.archive.org/web/20260826113039/https://paperspast.natlib.govt.nz/newspapers/CHP18630613.2.4): Direct archive retrieval failed in the sandbox (CONNECT 403); exact cited capture opened in the in-app browser and complete correctable-text tab read. Locator: Complete 1863 letter, paragraphs beginning We have often heard this debated, The fact is and NOTE; Gutenberg header and revised-edition preface. Passage (paraphrase): Human beings add power and self-acting mechanisms; machine reproduction still depends on people. The revised preface discusses the second Press article without supplying its title or date.
- [source-erewhon-1901-gutenberg](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/1906/pg1906.txt): Direct Node retrieval failed (DNS); complete cited text opened in the in-app browser; header, prefaces and editorial footnotes inspected. Locator: Complete 1863 letter, paragraphs beginning We have often heard this debated, The fact is and NOTE; Gutenberg header and revised-edition preface. Passage (paraphrase): Human beings add power and self-acting mechanisms; machine reproduction still depends on people. The revised preface discusses the second Press article without supplying its title or date.

### 2. `ts-a1-butler-1863-context` — absence holds

The letter explicitly places the addition of machinery’s power and self-regulation in human contrivance and its selection in human activity. Machinery making machinery is discussed as reproduction/manufacture, without a design-feedback argument.

Assertions under review: No machine improves itself or designs better machines in this letter. (qualification)

- [source-butler-1863-press](https://web.archive.org/web/20260826113039/https://paperspast.natlib.govt.nz/newspapers/CHP18630613.2.4): Exact cited archive capture opened in the in-app browser after direct HTTP failure; complete letter read. Locator: Complete letter, paragraphs beginning We have often heard this debated and The fact is; appended NOTE. Passage (paraphrase): The letter describes people creating successors and improving mechanical life, with machines dependent on people for continuation of their species.

### 3. `ts-a4-butler-1863-context` — absence holds

The letter describes development over ages and days of machine courtship as remote. Its relative comparison with biological development does not state a short transition after human-level capability.

Assertions under review: No short transition to superhuman machines is stated. (qualification)

- [source-butler-1863-press](https://web.archive.org/web/20260826113039/https://paperspast.natlib.govt.nz/newspapers/CHP18630613.2.4): Exact cited archive capture opened in the in-app browser after direct HTTP failure; complete letter read. Locator: Complete letter, paragraphs on successors and reproductive organs. Passage (paraphrase): The transition is placed over ages; the account of machines gaining ground supplies no bounded short takeoff period.

### 4. `ts-a5-butler-1863-context` — absence holds

The manufacturing passage is embedded in a discussion of reproduction. The surrounding paragraphs assign invention and improvements to people rather than machinery performing research or design.

Assertions under review: Machinery begetting machinery is described without machine research or design. (qualification)

- [source-butler-1863-press](https://web.archive.org/web/20260826113039/https://paperspast.natlib.govt.nz/newspapers/CHP18630613.2.4): Exact cited archive capture opened in the in-app browser after direct HTTP failure; complete letter read. Locator: Complete letter, paragraph beginning The fact is; appended NOTE. Passage (paraphrase): Existing machinery helps produce machinery of its kind; people supply improvements and selection.

### 6. `ts-b2-erewhon-1872-context` — scope issue

The cited paragraph expressly limits the mediation claim to the present and immediately considers machines acquiring direct hearing and developed language. The qualification drops that temporal and sensory scope and makes it sound like a limit throughout the fictional treatise.

Assertions under review: The machines act only through people. (qualification)

- [source-erewhon-1872-scan](https://archive.org/details/ErewhonOverrang00Butl/page/197): Internet Archive item metadata and official DjVu text derivative retrieved successfully by direct HTTPS; 1872 Trübner printing identified from title page, machinery chapters opened beyond the cited paragraph. OCR has errors. Retrieved version: https://archive.org/download/ErewhonOverrang00Butl/ErewhonOverrang00Butl_djvu.txt. Locator: 1872 printing, chapter XXI, printed pp. 196–197; paragraph beginning As yet the machines receive their impressions. Passage (quote): As yet the machines receive their impressions through the agency of man’s senses

Proposed wording: The cited paragraph describes present machine signals as mediated by human drivers, then considers future machines hearing directly and developing language.

### 7. `ts-a2-turing-1947-context` — absence holds

The final two typescript pages link alteration of instructions to learning from experience and then explicitly require extensive training and human contact. They provide no engineered seed architecture for repeated improvement of the improvement process.

Assertions under review: The cited self-modification passage concerns learning rather than a seed for recursive growth. (qualification); The lecture warns against expecting extensive instruction tables without human training. (qualification)

- [source-turing-1947-amt-b-1](https://turingarchive.kings.cam.ac.uk/publications-lectures-and-talks-amtb/amt-b-1): Complete 31-page PDF retrieved from the live media URL embedded by the cited catalogue page. Closing pp. 23–24 rendered and visually read; full-file automatic OCR proved too damaged to certify the rest. The verdict is bounded to the cited closing discussion. Retrieved version: https://mediadepot.kings.cam.ac.uk/getmedia/25/3aba98a45870caf929342f032cd994f63c0557583c01548254049a5b45e4707961faef9d296e03c59ed624cadf5f06cf83c01085ec35e872c2614eece1618786. Locator: Typescript pp. 23–24 (PDF pages 30–31), closing discussion. Passage (paraphrase): A machine can alter its own instructions to learn, but extensive training and contact with human beings are still required.

### 8. `ts-c2-turing-1950` — absence holds

The exact printed p. 448 describes a machine programmed for playing the game deliberately inserting arithmetic mistakes to confuse an interrogator. The statement is scoped to that designed strategy and supplies no later independent-goal or post-game switch scenario.

Assertions under review: In the imitation-game mistake strategy, the machine has no concealed goal of its own and no post-game change is described. (qualification)

- [source-turing-1950-amt-b-19](https://turingarchive.kings.cam.ac.uk/publications-lectures-and-talks-amtb/amt-b-19): Complete live 28-page offprint PDF retrieved behind the cited catalogue page; printed p. 448 rendered and read, with learning section pp. 456–460 inspected separately. Locator: Mind, printed p. 448 (PDF page 16), paragraph beginning The claim that machines cannot make mistakes. Passage (paraphrase): The machine is programmed for the game and deliberately introduces mistakes that confuse the interrogator; the paragraph describes no subsequent behavioural switch.

### 9. `ts-a2-turing-1950-context` — absence holds

The self-programming paragraph says some purpose, without specifying redesign of intelligence or a recursive improvement architecture. The learning section develops education, experimenter selection and learned rules; it does not specify the seed-AI architecture required by A2.

Assertions under review: Self-programming is toward an unspecified purpose, rather than a specified seed architecture for redesigning intelligence. (qualification)

- [source-turing-1950-amt-b-19](https://turingarchive.kings.cam.ac.uk/publications-lectures-and-talks-amtb/amt-b-19): Complete live media offprint opened; printed pp. 448–449 and 456–460 rendered and visually inspected, including both stored evidence passages. ### 200. `rd-r01-goertzel-2015-superintelligence-fears` — absence holds

Read section 6, including the orthogonality discussion, the instrumental-convergence objections and the treacherous-turn discussion. The author argues about likely rather than merely possible goal/intelligence pairings and rejects probability claims from random-mind reasoning. He allows dangerous outcomes; the passage supplies no theorem or claim that intelligence itself ensures benevolence. The verdict is confined to that argument, not to all of the essay’s alternative proposals.

Assertions under review: It does not argue that intelligence brings benevolent goals. (qualification; absence holds)

- [source-rd-goertzel-2015-superintelligence-fears](https://jetpress.org/v25.2/goertzel.htm): Direct HTTP retrieval on 2026-11-02; cited webpage opened. Reading coverage specified in findings. Passage (paraphrase): Engineered and taught minds need not be random samples of possible minds; this challenges a probability inference while leaving dangerous outcomes possible.

### 318. `ht-w07-bostrom-2012-superintelligent-will` — scope issue

Read the orthogonality argument, complete cognitive-enhancement section and conclusion. The paper does not advocate intelligence growth as a final end, but section 2.3 expressly considers whether an agent values knowledge and understanding for their own sakes and why some enhancements may have no instrumental value. A blanket absence of discussion is too broad. This does not establish any coinage or global priority.

Assertions under review: States that final goals are independent of intelligence; it does not discuss the growth of intelligence as an end. Not evidence of coinage of the term. Earliest located means the earliest text found by the collection’s searches and the cross-family re-check of 2026-11-02, not a first statement. ### 347. `yk-y17-extropians-1998-12-06-yudkowsky-2153-c` — absence holds

Read the complete 1998 message and its quoted debate. The truck metaphor opposes delay in a discussion of moral convergence and imposed goal systems, not a worked analysis of regulation or enforcement. The selected negative holds at that scope. The qualification’s later historical comparison is not treated as evidence that the 1998 message discusses regulation.

Assertions under review: Premise addressed: Law can prevent the danger (the part he states himself, as an enforced worldwide halt). Not about regulation as such. In 1998 he wanted the Singularity reached as fast as possible (‘Build superintelligence as fast as possible’, Y22). He later argues for an enforced, indefinite, worldwide halt (TIME, 2023; ‘Only Law Can Prevent Extinction’, 2026): he keeps the view that partial measures fail but reverses the conclusion that stopping is impossible or undesirable. A 1998–99 argument against delay, made when he expected a superintelligence to converge on correct morality; his 2023 call for an enforced worldwide halt (in TIME) reverses it. (qualification; absence holds)
